Head pruning reduces the number of heads contributing to cumulative importance scores, flattening the score distribution: the Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen) across all sparsity levels, with consistent negative deltas in all 16 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}). When importance scores lose discriminability, thresholding degenerates into noise-sensitive selection---functionally approaching biased random eviction. This effect is phase-dependent: in low-sparsity phases, attention is already broadly distributed, so pruning-induced flattening has maximum impact; in high-sparsity phases, concentration is preserved. Random eviction is immune because its decisions are independent of score quality, explaining $G \\approx 1$. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen",
          "+\\subsection{Discrimination Collapse}",
          "+\\label{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}",
          " ",
          "-Beyond discrimination collapse, head pruning redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads. For DeepSeek, the entropy delta is consistently negative ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} \\in [-0.047, -0.040]$), indicating that importance-based pruning systematically selects heads whose removal concentrates attention in survivors. For Qwen, the pattern partially replicates at $s \\leq 0.20$ but reverses at $s{=}0.25$ ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} = +0.045$), suggesting model-specific head specialization patterns (Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}). We characterize discrimination collapse as the robust, model-independent driver, with entropy redistribution as a secondary, model-dependent amplifier. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen",
          "+We observe that head pruning systematically reduces the discriminative power of cumulative importance scores, suggesting a mechanism for the phase-strategy interaction. The Gini coefficient of importance scores decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas in all 16 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}). This pattern---which we term \\emph{discrimination collapse}---is consistent with importance-based eviction degenerating into noise-sensitive selection when score distributions flatten. The effect is phase-dependent: in low-sparsity phases, attention is already broadly distributed, so pruning-induced flattening has maximum impact; in high-sparsity phases, concentration is preserved. This observation is independently corroborated by \\cite{hies-2025}, who report importance score collapse under heavy single-axis pruning; our results extend this to the compound setting, where collapse is amplified by the interaction of two compression axes and modulated by reasoning phase. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen",
          "+",
          "+A partial counterfactual supports this interpretation: random eviction does not rely on importance scores, so it is immune to score-distribution changes caused by head pruning. The flat severity gradient under random eviction ($G \\approx 1$) is consistent with this independence, while importance-based eviction---whose decisions depend on the very scores that pruning degrades---exhibits the phase-modulated amplification. We note that this evidence is correlational; causal validation through controlled manipulation of importance-score entropy is an important direction for future work. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen",
          "+",
          "+\\subsection{Entropy Redistribution}",
          "+\\label{sec:entropy-redistribution-main}",
          "+",
          "+Beyond discrimination collapse, we observe that head pruning redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads, though this effect is model-dependent. For DeepSeek, the entropy delta is consistently negative ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} \\in [-0.047, -0.040]$), suggesting that importance-based pruning selects heads whose removal concentrates attention in survivors. For Qwen, the pattern partially replicates at $s \\leq 0.20$ but reverses at $s{=}0.25$ ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} = +0.045$), with 22/30 problems showing positive entropy change (Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}). We characterize discrimination collapse as the robust, model-independent correlate, with entropy redistribution as a secondary, model-dependent factor whose role requires further investigation. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen"

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis: Entropy-Discrimination Collapse}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nWe propose \\emph{discrimination collapse} as the primary mechanism driving the phase-strategy interaction. Head pruning reduces the number of heads contributing to cumulative importance scores, flattening the score distribution: the Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen) across all sparsity levels, with consistent negative deltas in all 16 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}). When importance scores lose discriminability, thresholding degenerates into noise-sensitive selection---functionally approaching biased random eviction. This effect is phase-dependent: in low-sparsity phases, attention is already broadly distributed, so pruning-induced flattening has maximum impact; in high-sparsity phases, concentration is preserved. Random eviction is immune because its decisions are independent of score quality, explaining $G \\approx 1$. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\nBeyond discrimination collapse, head pruning redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads. For DeepSeek, the entropy delta is consistently negative ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} \\in [-0.047, -0.040]$), indicating that importance-based pruning systematically selects heads whose removal concentrates attention in survivors. For Qwen, the pattern partially replicates at $s \\leq 0.20$ but reverses at $s{=}0.25$ ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} = +0.045$), suggesting model-specific head specialization patterns (Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}). We characterize discrimination collapse as the robust, model-independent driver, with entropy redistribution as a secondary, model-dependent amplifier. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Compound compression.}\nCombining multiple compression axes produces non-additive errors.\n\\cite{spark-aaai2026} report super-additive degradation when combining channel pruning with token eviction; \\cite{slim-icml2025} formalize compound error for quantization and sparsity.\n\\cite{unicomp-2026} evaluate compound compression across 40+ benchmarks, revealing that reasoning capabilities degrade disproportionately.\n\\cite{phase-transition-compression-2026} characterize independent phase transition points per compression dimension, implying the joint safe region is the intersection of per-dimension safe zones.\nGRASPrune~\\cite{graspune-acl2026} learns global budget-constrained gates for joint pruning but treats decisions as static across generation.\nThese works establish compound interaction but share an implicit assumption: interaction magnitude is constant across the generation process.\n\n\\paragraph{KV cache eviction.}\nMost methods rely on importance scoring whose reliability is increasingly questioned.\nAda-KV~\\cite{adakv-neurips2025} introduces per-head adaptive budgets; SnapKV~\\cite{snapkv-neurips2024} compresses from a fixed observation window; \\cite{defensivekv-iclr2026} show worst-case loss $2.3$--$4.3{\\times}$ higher than expected under standard aggregation.\n\\cite{nacl-acl2024} combine proxy-token and random eviction to counter attention bias.\nRetentiveKV~\\cite{retentivekv-2026} identifies deferred importance, and \\cite{greedy-bias-kvcache-2026} show that single importance metrics exhibit systematic greedy bias.\n\\cite{hies-2025} independently show that importance-based head discrimination collapses under heavy pruning, further questioning single-metric reliability.\nLethe~\\cite{lethe-aaai2026} introduces temporal adaptivity with layer-wise multi-round pruning, and SparK~\\cite{spark-aaai2026} extends eviction to the channel dimension with recoverable sparsity.\nThese methods each optimize a single pruning axis---whether aggregation, metric, temporal, or channel---without characterizing compound interactions when multiple mechanisms are simultaneously active.\n\n\\paragraph{Phase-aware inference.}\nThinKV~\\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} applies different KV strategies per phase but without compound interaction.\n\\cite{decision-repr-transition-2026} find pruning sensitivity depends on ``silent'' vs.\\ ``decisive'' representation phases.\n\\cite{doing-more-with-less-2026} show pruning combined with test-time scaling can exceed unpruned baselines; \\cite{sparse-frontier-2025} find no universally optimal sparse attention strategy across generation stages.\n\\cite{physics-kvcache-2026} identify a sharp phase transition in single-axis KV cache compression at high sparsity, characterizing the single-dimension failure mode that our compound analysis extends across multiple axes.\nR-KV~\\cite{rkv-neurips2025} exploits reasoning-specific redundancy for aggressive cache compression, targeting per-axis optimization rather than characterizing compound degradation.\nThese works establish phase-dependent compression tolerance along single axes.\nOur work bridges this by quantifying how compound severity varies across phases and eviction strategies.\n",

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/docs/paper/limitations.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark. Different reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark. MATH-500 is a suitable first testbed because it elicits structured reasoning with clear phase transitions (exploratory vs.\\ structured computation), supports per-problem evaluation enabling paired statistical tests, and spans sufficient difficulty to produce meaningful variation in sequence length and attention dynamics. However, different reasoning tasks---code generation (e.g., HumanEval, LiveCodeBench), science reasoning (e.g., GPQA), multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile. Whether the phase-strategy interaction documented here extends to these domains is an open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Symmetric sparsity assumption.}\nOur analysis applies symmetric sparsity rates: the same fraction $s$ is used for both head pruning and KV cache eviction. Practical deployments may use asymmetric budgets (e.g., aggressive head pruning with conservative cache eviction, or vice versa). The interaction dynamics under asymmetric compound sparsity may differ from the symmetric case studied here; investigating asymmetric compound interactions is a natural extension."
}
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Discrimination Collapse Details}
2	\label{app:discrimination-collapse-details}
3	
4	This cross-model consistency (Table~\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}) establishes discrimination collapse as a robust consequence of compound sparsity. When importance scores lose discriminative power, thresholding the bottom-$s$ fraction becomes noise-sensitive: small perturbations change which positions cross the eviction boundary. In the limit of perfectly uniform scores, importance-based eviction degenerates into an arbitrary selection functionally equivalent to biased random eviction. The same pattern holds under random eviction (DeepSeek: $-0.031$ to $-0.073$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.045$), confirming that discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen
5	
6	
7	\section{Extended Discussion}
8	\label{app:extended-discussion}
9	
10	\paragraph{On the predictability of phase-modulated severity.}
11	The compound interaction is not uniformly fragile---it is selectively fragile in a phase-dependent manner ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\text{high}} < 0.06$ at $s \leq 0.30$; at $s = 0.35$, high-phase II rises to $0.105$ in a supplementary experiment, indicating onset of compound interaction even in high-sparsity phases at extreme pruning rates). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_imp_s035; high-phase medians: 0.006, 0.015, 0.032, 0.053; s035 II_high=0.1051
12	
13	\paragraph{Random masking as falsification control.}
14	Random eviction fails 7/8 paired permutation tests across both models; the single exception (Qwen $s{=}0.30$, $p{=}0.012$) exhibits negligible effect size ($\widetilde{\Delta} = \placeholder{}$). The absence of a phase gradient under random masking isolates the amplification as strategy-specific. % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030, bf16_qw_rand_s015..s030
15	
16	\paragraph{Entropy mechanism boundary conditions.}
17	Directly measuring per-head entropy shifts across architecturally distinct models---and identifying what training-time properties determine whether entropy concentrates or disperses under head pruning---would clarify the boundary conditions of the full mechanism.
18	
19	
20	\section{Extended Limitations}
21	\label{app:limitations}
22	
23	\paragraph{Model scale.}
24	All experiments are conducted at the 7B parameter scale.
25	Whether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested.
26	Larger models may exhibit different attention redundancy structures that alter the discrimination collapse dynamics (and the model-dependent entropy component may behave differently at scale).
27	% REVIEWER-ATTACK: "7B is too small to be relevant for production."
28	% REBUTTAL: 7B is the most-deployed scale for on-device and cost-constrained serving—exactly where compound sparsity is needed. The mechanism (entropy-discrimination collapse) is scale-agnostic in principle. Scaling experiments are future work.
29	
30	\paragraph{Architectural scope.}
31	Our two models---DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct---share the Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.
32	The cross-model replication (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}) establishes that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, though both models share the same Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.
33	Preliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \S\ref{sec:cross-arch}) shows qualitatively consistent within-importance phase gradients ($d_z \sim 1.0$) but a substantially weaker strategy $\times$ phase interaction than in Qwen2-based architectures; random pruning also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient on LLaMA, reducing the strategy contrast. This partial confirmation ($s{=}0.30$ Wilcoxon $p_{\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$; $s \leq 0.25$ non-significant) suggests the qualitative pattern may generalize but quantitative impact is architecture-dependent. Comprehensive cross-architecture validation across more diverse designs (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention architectures) and scales remains an important next step.
34	% REVIEWER-ATTACK: "Cross-model ≠ cross-architecture. Both are Qwen2ForCausalLM."

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Compound sparsity**: Simultaneous attention head pruning + KV cache eviction
   - **Interaction Index (II)**: Measures super/sub-additivity; II = R_b - R_a - R_k + 1
   - **Severity gradient (G)**: Ratio of low-phase to high-phase median II (69×→274× in bf16)
   - **Median difference (Δ)**: Primary metric replacing ratio G; DeepSeek 0.39→14.59, Qwen 0.039→2.943
   - **Cohen's d_z**: Paired effect size; DeepSeek 0.44→0.80 (small→large), Qwen 0.28→1.02 (s015 ns)
   - **Phase segmentation**: Quantile-based partitioning into high/mid/low-sparsity phases
   - **Discrimination collapse**: Head pruning flattens importance scores (Gini decreases 0.030-0.081 DeepSeek, 0.010-0.020 Qwen across all 16 conditions)
   - **Entropy redistribution**: Secondary model-dependent factor (DeepSeek consistent, Qwen reverses at s=0.25)
   - **Crossover phenomenon**: Importance outperforms random in high-sparsity phase but underperforms in low-sparsity phase
   - **Framing constraint (D015)**: MUST say "emergent phase-strategy interaction", MUST NOT say "importance is broken"
   - **D033**: Deprecated ratio G as primary metric, replaced by median difference + Cohen's d
   - **LLaMA results**: ~1/40 magnitude of DeepSeek, 1/4 conditions significant, random also has phase gradient (d_z 0.35→0.44) → "preliminary/exploratory" framing

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe showed that compound sparsity severity---the super-additive interaction between head pruning and KV cache eviction---is not phase-invariant but exhibits an emergent phase-strategy interaction spanning over an order of magnitude within a single generation. Under importance-based eviction, the severity gradient grows from $69{\\times}$ to $274{\\times}$ across sparsity levels ($d_z$: $0.44 \\to 0.80$); random eviction shows no such modulation (7/8 conditions non-significant). A crossover at $s{=}0.25$---where importance-based eviction reverses ranking across phases---demonstrates that no single strategy dominates uniformly. The pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct at $s \\geq 0.20$ ($d_z \\geq 0.55$); suggestive preliminary evidence from DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM) indicates the phenomenon may extend beyond Qwen2, though at ${\\sim}1/40$ the magnitude and with a weaker strategy contrast. Discrimination collapse provides a correlational mechanistic explanation supported by both Qwen2-based models.\nThese findings motivate phase-aware sparsity allocation for reasoning model deployment. Extending this analysis to code reasoning (HumanEval, LiveCodeBench) and science reasoning (GPQA), validating the discrimination collapse mechanism through causal intervention, and characterizing asymmetric compound interactions are promising directions for future work.\n",
        "numLines": 6,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 6
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/conclusion.tex"
  },

  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/docs/paper/limitations.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/docs/paper/limitations.tex",
        "content": "\\paragraph{Model scale and architecture.}\nAll experiments are conducted at the 7--8B parameter scale. Whether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested. Preliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}) partially confirms the qualitative pattern beyond the Qwen2 architecture---monotonically increasing $d_z$ with within-importance phase gradient $\\sim$1.0---but with substantially weaker interaction magnitude ($\\sim$1/40 of DeepSeek) and mixed statistical evidence (only $s{=}0.30$ significant after Bonferroni correction). The pattern has been tested on two architecture families (Qwen2ForCausalLM, LlamaForCausalLM); comprehensive validation across more diverse architectures (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention) and larger scales is needed for full generalization claims.\n\n\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark. MATH-500 is a suitable first testbed because it elicits structured reasoning with clear phase transitions (exploratory vs.\\ structured computation), supports per-problem evaluation enabling paired statistical tests, and spans sufficient difficulty to produce meaningful variation in sequence length and attention dynamics. However, different reasoning tasks---code generation (e.g., HumanEval, LiveCodeBench), science reasoning (e.g., GPQA), multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile. Whether the phase-strategy interaction documented here extends to these domains is an open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Symmetric sparsity assumption.}\nOur analysis applies symmetric sparsity rates: the same fraction $s$ is used for both head pruning and KV cache eviction. Practical deployments may use asymmetric budgets (e.g., aggressive head pruning with conservative cache eviction, or vice versa). The interaction dynamics under asymmetric compound sparsity may differ from the symmetric case studied here; investigating asymmetric compound interactions is a natural extension.\n\n\\paragraph{Mechanism.}\nThe discrimination collapse mechanism is supported by correlational evidence (consistent Gini/CV reduction across all conditions); causal validation through controlled entropy manipulation is needed. The entropy redistribution component replicates on only 2/4 Qwen conditions, suggesting model-specific factors.\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity range.}\nOur experiments span $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$. More extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics. We do not report inference throughput or latency measurements, which are necessary for a complete deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization. Extended limitations in Appendix~\\ref{app:limitations}.\n",
        "numLines": 15,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 15
      }
    },

  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Compound compression.}\nCombining multiple compression axes produces non-additive errors.\n\\cite{spark-aaai2026} report super-additive degradation when combining channel pruning with token eviction; \\cite{slim-icml2025} formalize compound error for quantization and sparsity.\n\\cite{unicomp-2026} evaluate compound compression across 40+ benchmarks, revealing that reasoning capabilities degrade disproportionately.\n\\cite{phase-transition-compression-2026} characterize independent phase transition points per compression dimension, implying the joint safe region is the intersection of per-dimension safe zones.\nGRASPrune~\\cite{graspune-acl2026} learns global budget-constrained gates for joint pruning but treats decisions as static across generation.\nThese works establish compound interaction but share an implicit assumption: interaction magnitude is constant across the generation process.\n\n\\paragraph{KV cache eviction.}\nMost methods rely on importance scoring whose reliability is increasingly questioned.\nAda-KV~\\cite{adakv-neurips2025} introduces per-head adaptive budgets; SnapKV~\\cite{snapkv-neurips2024} compresses from a fixed observation window; \\cite{defensivekv-iclr2026} show worst-case loss $2.3$--$4.3{\\times}$ higher than expected under standard aggregation.\n\\cite{nacl-acl2024} combine proxy-token and random eviction to counter attention bias.\nRetentiveKV~\\cite{retentivekv-2026} identifies deferred importance, and \\cite{greedy-bias-kvcache-2026} show that single importance metrics exhibit systematic greedy bias.\n\\cite{hies-2025} independently show that importance-based head discrimination collapses under heavy single-axis pruning. Our work extends this to the compound setting, revealing that collapse is amplified by the interaction of two compression axes and modulated by reasoning phase---phenomena absent in single-axis analysis. The crossover effect (\\S\\ref{sec:crossover}) is a prediction that single-axis frameworks cannot generate.\nLethe~\\cite{lethe-aaai2026} introduces temporal adaptivity with layer-wise multi-round pruning, and SparK~\\cite{spark-aaai2026} extends eviction to the channel dimension with recoverable sparsity.\nThese methods each optimize a single pruning axis---whether aggregation, metric, temporal, or channel---without characterizing compound interactions when multiple mechanisms are simultaneously active.\n\n\\paragraph{Phase-aware inference.}\nThinKV~\\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} applies different KV strategies per phase but without compound interaction.\n\\cite{decision-repr-transition-2026} find pruning sensitivity depends on ``silent'' vs.\\ ``decisive'' representation phases.\n\\cite{doing-more-with-less-2026} show pruning combined with test-time scaling can exceed unpruned baselines; \\cite{sparse-frontier-2025} find no universally optimal sparse attention strategy across generation stages.\n\\cite{physics-kvcache-2026} identify a sharp phase transition in single-axis KV cache compression at high sparsity, characterizing the single-dimension failure mode that our compound analysis extends across multiple axes.\nR-KV~\\cite{rkv-neurips2025} exploits reasoning-specific redundancy for aggressive cache compression, targeting per-axis optimization rather than characterizing compound degradation.\nThese works establish phase-dependent compression tolerance along single axes.\nOur work bridges this by quantifying how compound severity varies across phases and eviction strategies.\n",
        "numLines": 29,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 29
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/related_work.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",

  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/docs/paper/analysis.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/docs/paper/analysis.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Discrimination Collapse under Compound Sparsity}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\subsection{Discrimination Collapse}\n\\label{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}\n\nWe observe that head pruning systematically reduces the discriminative power of cumulative importance scores, suggesting a mechanism for the phase-strategy interaction. The Gini coefficient of importance scores decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas in all 16 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}). This pattern---which we term \\emph{discrimination collapse}---is consistent with importance-based eviction degenerating into noise-sensitive selection when score distributions flatten. The effect is phase-dependent: in low-sparsity phases, attention is already broadly distributed, so pruning-induced flattening has maximum impact; in high-sparsity phases, concentration is preserved. This observation is independently corroborated by \\cite{hies-2025}, who report importance score collapse under heavy single-axis pruning; our results extend this to the compound setting, where collapse is amplified by the interaction of two compression axes and modulated by reasoning phase. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\nA partial counterfactual supports this interpretation: random eviction does not rely on importance scores, so it is immune to score-distribution changes caused by head pruning. The flat severity gradient under random eviction ($G \\approx 1$) is consistent with this independence, while importance-based eviction---whose decisions depend on the very scores that pruning degrades---exhibits the phase-modulated amplification. We note that this evidence is correlational; causal validation through controlled manipulation of importance-score entropy is an important direction for future work. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\n\\subsection{Entropy Redistribution}\n\\label{sec:entropy-redistribution-main}\n\nBeyond discrimination collapse, we observe that head pruning redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads, though this effect is model-dependent. For DeepSeek, the entropy delta is consistently negative ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} \\in [-0.047, -0.040]$), suggesting that importance-based pruning selects heads whose removal concentrates attention in survivors. For Qwen, the pattern partially replicates at $s \\leq 0.20$ but reverses at $s{=}0.25$ ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} = +0.045$), with 22/30 problems showing positive entropy change (Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}). We characterize discrimination collapse as the robust, model-independent correlate, with entropy redistribution as a secondary, model-dependent factor whose role requires further investigation. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n",
        "numLines": 15,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 15
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/analysis.tex"
  },

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nCompound sparsity---simultaneously pruning attention heads and evicting KV cache entries---is essential for efficient LLM inference but produces super-additive quality degradation whose structure is poorly understood.\nWe show that compound severity is not phase-invariant: it is an emergent phase-strategy interaction that varies by over two orders of magnitude across reasoning phases in mathematical reasoning models.\n\nOn DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (50 MATH-500 problems, $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$), importance-based eviction produces a monotonically increasing severity gradient from the high-sparsity to low-sparsity reasoning phase (median $\\Delta$: $0.32 \\to 9.94$; Cohen's $d_z$: $0.44 \\to 0.80$; permutation $p < 0.005$ at all levels), while random eviction shows no such modulation (7/8 conditions non-significant).\nA crossover emerges at $s{=}0.25$: importance-based eviction outperforms random in high-sparsity phases yet underperforms in low-sparsity phases---a qualitative reversal in strategy ranking.\nThe pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct at $s \\geq 0.20$, with preliminary cross-architecture evidence from a LlamaForCausalLM model suggesting the phenomenon may extend beyond Qwen2-family architectures.\nWe observe that discrimination collapse---head pruning flattening cumulative importance scores---correlates with the phase-dependent severity amplification, suggesting a mechanism whereby compound sparsity selectively degrades importance-based eviction in distributed-attention phases.\nThese findings motivate phase-aware compression budgets for mathematical reasoning models.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 11,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 11
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/abstract.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",
  "uuid": "c3b1eb3c-2304-40d4-a22e-b07978eb035e",
  "timestamp": "2026-05-15T08:21:45.764Z",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BWz3JFfkP3u4DvC3N5Qg9W`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discrimination Collapse Details}
2	\label{app:discrimination-collapse-details}
3	
4	This cross-model consistency (Table~\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}) establishes discrimination collapse as a robust consequence of compound sparsity. When importance scores lose discriminative power, thresholding the bottom-$s$ fraction becomes noise-sensitive: small perturbations change which positions cross the eviction boundary. In the limit of perfectly uniform scores, importance-based eviction degenerates into an arbitrary selection functionally equivalent to biased random eviction. The same pattern holds under random eviction (DeepSeek: $-0.031$ to $-0.073$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.045$), confirming that discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen
5	
6	
7	\section{Extended Discussion}
8	\label{app:extended-discussion}
9	
10	\paragraph{On the predictability of phase-modulated severity.}
11	The compound interaction is not uniformly fragile---it is selectively fragile in a phase-dependent manner ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\text{high}} < 0.06$ at $s \leq 0.30$; at $s = 0.35$, high-phase II rises to $0.105$ in a supplementary experiment, indicating onset of compound interaction even in high-sparsity phases at extreme pruning rates). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_imp_s035; high-phase medians: 0.006, 0.015, 0.032, 0.053; s035 II_high=0.1051
12	
13	\paragraph{Random masking as falsification control.}
14	Random eviction fails 7/8 paired permutation tests across both models; the single exception (Qwen $s{=}0.30$, $p{=}0.012$) exhibits negligible effect size ($\widetilde{\Delta} = \placeholder{}$). The absence of a phase gradient under random masking isolates the amplification as strategy-specific. % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030, bf16_qw_rand_s015..s030
15	
16	\paragraph{Entropy mechanism boundary conditions.}
17	Directly measuring per-head entropy shifts across architecturally distinct models---and identifying what training-time properties determine whether entropy concentrates or disperses under head pruning---would clarify the boundary conditions of the full mechanism.
18	
19	
20	\section{Extended Limitations}
21	\label{app:limitations}
22	
23	\paragraph{Model scale.}
24	All experiments are conducted at the 7B parameter scale.
25	Whether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested.
26	Larger models may exhibit different attention redundancy structures that alter the discrimination collapse dynamics (and the model-dependent entropy component may behave differently at scale).
27	% REVIEWER-ATTACK: "7B is too small to be relevant for production."
28	% REBUTTAL: 7B is the most-deployed scale for on-device and cost-constrained serving—exactly where compound sparsity is needed. The mechanism (entropy-discrimination collapse) is scale-agnostic in principle. Scaling experiments are future work.
29	
30	\paragraph{Architectural scope.}
31	Our two models---DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct---share the Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.
32	The cross-model replication (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}) establishes that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, though both models share the same Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.
33	Preliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \S\ref{sec:cross-arch}) shows qualitatively consistent within-importance phase gradients ($d_z \sim 1.0$) but a substantially weaker strategy $\times$ phase interaction than in Qwen2-based architectures; random pruning also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient on LLaMA, reducing the strategy contrast. This partial confirmation ($s{=}0.30$ Wilcoxon $p_{\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$; $s \leq 0.25$ non-significant) suggests the qualitative pattern may generalize but quantitative impact is architecture-dependent. Comprehensive cross-architecture validation across more diverse designs (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention architectures) and scales remains an important next step.
34	% REVIEWER-ATTACK: "Cross-model ≠ cross-architecture. Both are Qwen2ForCausalLM."
35	% REBUTTAL: We now include cross-architecture validation on LlamaForCausalLM (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}), which shows a qualitatively consistent but substantially weaker pattern. We use qualified language ("partially supports") rather than claiming full generalization.
36	
37	\paragraph{Task coverage.}
38	We evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark.
39	Different reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.
40	Generalizing the phase-strategy interaction to broader reasoning domains is an important next step.
41	% REVIEWER-ATTACK: "Only MATH-500. How do we know this isn't a math-specific artifact?"
42	% REBUTTAL: The mechanism (entropy-discrimination collapse under head pruning) depends on attention distribution properties, not math-specific features. MATH-500 is a standard benchmark with diverse difficulty. However, different reasoning tasks may induce different phase structures—we state this limitation explicitly rather than overclaiming.
43	
44	\paragraph{Statistical scope.}
45	Bootstrap confidence intervals use $B{=}10{,}000$ resamples (recomputed from initial $B{=}1{,}000$ profiling runs). The paired permutation test yields $n{=}49$ paired problems for DeepSeek (1 excluded) and $n{=}46$ for Qwen (4 excluded). % exp_id: cohens_d_bf16.json, cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json
46	The severity difference distribution is right-skewed (mean/median ratio ${\sim}1.6{:}1$ at $s{=}0.30$), motivating our use of medians and nonparametric tests rather than parametric alternatives. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030; mean_diff=22.94, median_diff=14.59 (segment-level). Per-problem paired: median_diff=9.9436 at s030
47	All four permutation tests reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$; Table~\ref{tab:permutation}), with CI lower bounds increasing from $0.19$ to $6.70$---confirming the effect direction at every sparsity level. The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification. % exp_id: permutation_test_results.json; permutation CIs: s015=[0.19,0.62], s020=[0.69,1.49], s025=[1.66,6.64], s030=[6.70,16.29]
48	
49	A notable exception among the random baselines is the Qwen random condition at $s{=}0.30$, which yields a statistically significant result ($p{=}0.012$) despite negligible effect size ($\widetilde{\Delta} = \placeholder{}$, bootstrap CI spanning zero). % exp_id: qw_rand_s030; B=10000, p=0.012, d=-0.035, negligible
50	This case illustrates that dichotomous significance testing can flag effects of no practical magnitude---reinforcing the importance of reporting effect sizes and confidence intervals alongside $p$-values.
51	
52	\paragraph{Sparsity range.}
53	Our experiments span $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$, a moderate range representative of practical deployment targets.
54	More extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.
55	Conversely, the lower boundary at which phase-modulated amplification first emerges is not yet characterized.
56	% REVIEWER-ATTACK: "Why not test higher sparsity? 0.30 is conservative."
57	% REBUTTAL: Our focus is on the practically relevant regime where models retain usable quality. At s>0.30 both strategies degrade severely, making II measurement less informative. The sub-exponential growth (C6, $\Delta$ growth rate $3.8{\times} \to 4.8{\times} \to 2.0{\times}$) suggests saturation at higher sparsity, but confirming this is future work.
58	
59	\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}
60	We do not report inference throughput or latency, which are necessary for deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization.
61	% REVIEWER-ATTACK: "No system evaluation. How do we know the severity amplification matters for real deployment?"
62	% REBUTTAL: This is an empirical finding paper, not a method/system paper. The contribution is characterizing *what happens* (phase-modulated severity) and *why* (entropy-discrimination collapse), not building a system to fix it. Phase-gated controllers are explicitly scoped as future work. The finding is actionable regardless: any compound sparsity deployment should be aware of phase-dependent severity.
63	
64	% Entropy redistribution analysis moved to main text \S\ref{sec:entropy-redistribution}
65	
```

Scaling experiments are future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Architectural scope.}\nOur two models---DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct---share the Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nThe cross-model replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) establishes that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, though both models share the same Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nPreliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}) shows qualitatively consistent within-importance phase gradients ($d_z \\sim 1.0$) but a substantially weaker strategy $\\times$ phase interaction than in Qwen2-based architectures; random pruning also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient on LLaMA, reducing the strategy contrast. This partial confirmation ($s{=}0.30$ Wilcoxon $p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$; $s \\leq 0.25$ non-significant) suggests the qualitative pattern may generalize but quantitative impact is architecture-dependent. Comprehensive cross-architecture validation across more diverse designs (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention architectures) and scales remains an important next step.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"Cross-model ≠ cross-architecture. Both are Qwen2ForCausalLM.\"\n% REBUTTAL: We now include cross-architecture validation on LlamaForCausalLM (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}), which shows a qualitatively consistent but substantially weaker pattern. We use qualified language (\"partially supports\") rather than claiming full generalization.\n\n\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark.\nDifferent reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.\nGeneralizing the phase-strategy interaction to broader reasoning domains is an important next step.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"Only MATH-500. How do we know this isn't a math-specific artifact?\"\n% REBUTTAL: The mechanism (entropy-discrimination collapse under head pruning) depends on attention distribution properties, not math-specific features. MATH-500 is a standard benchmark with diverse difficulty. However, different reasoning tasks may induce different phase structures—we state this limitation explicitly rather than overclaiming.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical scope.}\nBootstrap confidence intervals use $B{=}10{,}000$ resamples (recomputed from initial $B{=}1{,}000$ profiling runs). The paired permutation test yields $n{=}49$ paired problems for DeepSeek (1 excluded) and $n{=}46$ for Qwen (4 excluded). % exp_id: cohens_d_bf16.json, cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\nThe severity difference distribution is right-skewed (mean/median ratio ${\\sim}1.6{:}1$ at $s{=}0.30$), motivating our use of medians and nonparametric tests rather than parametric alternatives. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030; mean_diff=22.94, median_diff=14.59 (segment-level). Per-problem paired: median_diff=9.9436 at s030\nAll four permutation tests reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$; Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}), with CI lower bounds increasing from $0.19$ to $6.70$---confirming the effect direction at every sparsity level. The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification. % exp_id: permutation_test_results.json; permutation CIs: s015=[0.19,0.62], s020=[0.69,1.49], s025=[1.66,6.64], s030=[6.70,16.29]\n\nA notable exception among the random baselines is the Qwen random condition at $s{=}0.30$, which yields a statistically significant result ($p{=}0.012$) despite negligible effect size ($\\widetilde{\\Delta} = \\placeholder{}$, bootstrap CI spanning zero). % exp_id: qw_rand_s030; B=10000, p=0.012, d=-0.035, negligible\nThis case illustrates that dichotomous significance testing can flag effects of no practical magnitude---reinforcing the importance of reporting effect sizes and confidence intervals alongside $p$-values.\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity range.}\nOur experiments span $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$, a moderate range representative of practical deployment targets.\nMore extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.\nConversely, the lower boundary at which phase-modulated amplification first emerges is not yet characterized.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"Why not test higher sparsity? 0.30 is conservative.\"\n% REBUTTAL: Our focus is on the practically relevant regime where models retain usable quality. At s>0.30 both strategies degrade severely, making II measurement less informative. The sub-exponential growth (C6, $\\Delta$ growth rate $3.8{\\times} \\to 4.8{\\times} \\to 2.0{\\times}$) suggests saturation at higher sparsity, but confirming this is future work.\n\n\\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}\nWe do not report inference throughput or latency, which are necessary for deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"No system evaluation. How do we know the severity amplification matters for real deployment?\"\n% REBUTTAL: This is an empirical finding paper, not a method/system paper. The contribution is characterizing *what happens* (phase-modulated severity) and *why* (entropy-discrimination collapse), not building a system to fix it. Phase-gated controllers are explicitly scoped as future work. The finding is actionable regardless: any compound sparsity deployment should be aware of phase-dependent severity.\n\n% Entropy redistribution analysis moved to main text \\S\\ref{sec:entropy-redistribution}\n",
      "numLines": 65,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 65
    }
  },

    "oldString": "The compound interaction is not uniformly fragile---it is selectively fragile in a phase-dependent manner ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\text{high}} < 0.06$ at $s \\leq 0.30$; at $s = 0.35$, high-phase II rises to $0.105$ in a supplementary experiment, indicating onset of compound interaction even in high-sparsity phases at extreme pruning rates). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_imp_s035; high-phase medians: 0.006, 0.015, 0.032, 0.053; s035 II_high=0.1051",
    "newString": "The compound interaction is not uniformly fragile---it is selectively fragile in a phase-dependent manner ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\text{high}} < 0.06$ at $s \\leq 0.30$). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; high-phase medians: 0.006, 0.015, 0.032, 0.053",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discrimination Collapse Details}\n\\label{app:discrimination-collapse-details}\n\nThis cross-model consistency (Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}) establishes discrimination collapse as a robust consequence of compound sparsity. When importance scores lose discriminative power, thresholding the bottom-$s$ fraction becomes noise-sensitive: small perturbations change which positions cross the eviction boundary. In the limit of perfectly uniform scores, importance-based eviction degenerates into an arbitrary selection functionally equivalent to biased random eviction. The same pattern holds under random eviction (DeepSeek: $-0.031$ to $-0.073$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.045$), confirming that discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \\emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\n\n\\section{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended-discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{On the predictability of phase-modulated severity.}\nThe compound interaction is not uniformly fragile---it is selectively fragile in a phase-dependent manner ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\text{high}} < 0.06$ at $s \\leq 0.30$; at $s = 0.35$, high-phase II rises to $0.105$ in a supplementary experiment, indicating onset of compound interaction even in high-sparsity phases at extreme pruning rates). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_imp_s035; high-phase medians: 0.006, 0.015, 0.032, 0.053; s035 II_high=0.1051\n\n\\paragraph{Random masking as falsification control.}\nRandom eviction fails 7/8 paired permutation tests across both models; the single exception (Qwen $s{=}0.30$, $p{=}0.012$) exhibits negligible effect size ($\\widetilde{\\Delta} = \\placeholder{}$). The absence of a phase gradient under random masking isolates the amplification as strategy-specific. % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030, bf16_qw_rand_s015..s030\n\n\\paragraph{Entropy mechanism boundary conditions.}\nDirectly measuring per-head entropy shifts across architecturally distinct models---and identifying what training-time properties determine whether entropy concentrates or disperses under head pruning---would clarify the boundary conditions of the full mechanism.\n\n\n\\section{Extended Limitations}\n\\label{app:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Model scale.}\nAll experiments are conducted at the 7B parameter scale.\nWhether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested.\nLarger models may exhibit different attention redundancy structures that alter the discrimination collapse dynamics (and the model-dependent entropy component may behave differently at scale).\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"7B is too small to be relevant for production.\"\n% REBUTTAL: 7B is the most-deployed scale for on-device and cost-constrained serving—exactly where compound sparsity is needed. The mechanism (entropy-discrimination collapse) is scale-agnostic in principle. Scaling experiments are future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Architectural scope.}\nOur two models---DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct---share the Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nThe cross-model replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) establishes that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, though both models share the same Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nPreliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}) shows qualitatively consistent within-importance phase gradients ($d_z \\sim 1.0$) but a substantially weaker strategy $\\times$ phase interaction than in Qwen2-based architectures; random pruning also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient on LLaMA, reducing the strategy contrast. This partial confirmation ($s{=}0.30$ Wilcoxon $p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$; $s \\leq 0.25$ non-significant) suggests the qualitative pattern may generalize but quantitative impact is architecture-dependent. Comprehensive cross-architecture validation across more diverse designs (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention architectures) and scales remains an important next step.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"Cross-model ≠ cross-architecture. Both are Qwen2ForCausalLM.\"\n% REBUTTAL: We now include cross-architecture validation on LlamaForCausalLM (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}), which shows a qualitatively consistent but substantially weaker pattern. We use qualified language (\"partially supports\") rather than claiming full generalization.\n\n\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark.\nDifferent reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.\nGeneralizing the phase-strategy interaction to broader reasoning domains is an important next step.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"Only MATH-500. How do we know this isn't a math-specific artifact?\"\n% REBUTTAL: The mechanism (entropy-discrimination collapse under head pruning) depends on attention distribution properties, not math-specific features. MATH-500 is a standard benchmark with diverse difficulty. However, different reasoning tasks may induce different phase structures—we state this limitation explicitly rather than overclaiming.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical scope.}\nBootstrap confidence intervals use $B{=}10{,}000$ resamples (recomputed from initial $B{=}1{,}000$ profiling runs). The paired permutation test yields $n{=}49$ paired problems for DeepSeek (1 excluded) and $n{=}46$ for Qwen (4 excluded). % exp_id: cohens_d_bf16.json, cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\nThe severity difference distribution is right-skewed (mean/median ratio ${\\sim}1.6{:}1$ at $s{=}0.30$), motivating our use of medians and nonparametric tests rather than parametric alternatives. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030; mean_diff=22.94, median_diff=14.59 (segment-level). Per-problem paired: median_diff=9.9436 at s030\nAll four permutation tests reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$; Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}), with CI lower bounds increasing from $0.19$ to $6.70$---confirming the effect direction at every sparsity level. The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification. % exp_id: permutation_test_results.json; permutation CIs: s015=[0.19,0.62], s020=[0.69,1.49], s025=[1.66,6.64], s030=[6.70,16.29]\n\nA notable exception among the random baselines is the Qwen random condition at $s{=}0.30$, which yields a statistically significant result ($p{=}0.012$) despite negligible effect size ($\\widetilde{\\Delta} = \\placeholder{}$, bootstrap CI spanning zero). % exp_id: qw_rand_s030; B=10000, p=0.012, d=-0.035, negligible\nThis case illustrates that dichotomous significance testing can flag effects of no practical magnitude---reinforcing the importance of reporting effect sizes and confidence intervals alongside $p$-values.\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity range.}\nOur experiments span $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$, a moderate range representative of practical deployment targets.\nMore extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.\nConversely, the lower boundary at which phase-modulated amplification first emerges is not yet characterized.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"Why not test higher sparsity? 0.30 is conservative.\"\n% REBUTTAL: Our focus is on the practically relevant regime where models retain usable quality. At s>0.30 both strategies degrade severely, making II measurement less informative. The sub-exponential growth (C6, $\\Delta$ growth rate $3.8{\\times} \\to 4.8{\\times} \\to 2.0{\\times}$) suggests saturation at higher sparsity, but confirming this is future work.\n\n\\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}\nWe do not report inference throughput or latency, which are necessary for deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"No system evaluation. How do we know the severity amplification matters for real deployment?\"\n% REBUTTAL: This is an empirical finding paper, not a method/system paper. The contribution is characterizing *what happens* (phase-modulated severity) and *why* (entropy-discrimination collapse), not building a system to fix it. Phase-gated controllers are explicitly scoped as future work. The finding is actionable regardless: any compound sparsity deployment should be aware of phase-dependent severity.\n\n% Entropy redistribution analysis moved to main text \\S\\ref{sec:entropy-redistribution}\n",

The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification. % exp_id: permutation_test_results.json; permutation CIs: s015=[0.19,0.62], s020=[0.69,1.49], s025=[1.66,6.64], s030=[6.70,16.29]\n\nA notable exception among the random baselines is the Qwen random condition at $s{=}0.30$, which yields a statistically significant result ($p{=}0.012$) despite negligible effect size ($\\widetilde{\\Delta} = \\placeholder{}$, bootstrap CI spanning zero). % exp_id: qw_rand_s030; B=10000, p=0.012, d=-0.035, negligible\nThis case illustrates that dichotomous significance testing can flag effects of no practical magnitude---reinforcing the importance of reporting effect sizes and confidence intervals alongside $p$-values.\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity range.}\nOur experiments span $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$, a moderate range representative of practical deployment targets.\nMore extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.\nConversely, the lower boundary at which phase-modulated amplification first emerges is not yet characterized.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"Why not test higher sparsity? 0.30 is conservative.\"\n% REBUTTAL: Our focus is on the practically relevant regime where models retain usable quality. At s>0.30 both strategies degrade severely, making II measurement less informative. The sub-exponential growth (C6, $\\Delta$ growth rate $3.8{\\times} \\to 4.8{\\times} \\to 2.0{\\times}$) suggests saturation at higher sparsity, but confirming this is future work.\n\n\\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}\nWe do not report inference throughput or latency, which are necessary for deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"No system evaluation. How do we know the severity amplification matters for real deployment?\"\n% REBUTTAL: This is an empirical finding paper, not a method/system paper. The contribution is characterizing *what happens* (phase-modulated severity) and *why* (entropy-discrimination collapse), not building a system to fix it. Phase-gated controllers are explicitly scoped as future work. The finding is actionable regardless: any compound sparsity deployment should be aware of phase-dependent severity.\n\n% Entropy redistribution analysis moved to main text \\S\\ref{sec:entropy-redistribution}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discrimination Collapse Details}\n\\label{app:discrimination-collapse-details}\n\nThis cross-model consistency (Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}) establishes discrimination collapse as a robust consequence of compound sparsity. In the limit of perfectly uniform scores, importance-based eviction degenerates into an arbitrary selection functionally equivalent to biased random eviction. The absence of a phase gradient under random masking isolates the amplification as strategy-specific. % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030, bf16_qw_rand_s015..s030\n\n\\paragraph{Entropy mechanism boundary conditions.}\nDirectly measuring per-head entropy shifts across architecturally distinct models---and identifying what training-time properties determine whether entropy concentrates or disperses under head pruning---would clarify the boundary conditions of the full mechanism.\n\n\n\\section{Extended Limitations}\n\\label{app:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Model scale.}\nAll experiments are conducted at the 7B parameter scale.\nWhether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested.\nLarger models may exhibit different attention redundancy structures that alter the discrimination collapse dynamics (and the model-dependent entropy component may behave differently at scale).\n\n\n\\paragraph{Architectural scope.}\nOur two models---DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct---share the Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nThe cross-model replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) establishes that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, though both models share the same Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nPreliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}) shows qualitatively consistent within-importance phase gradients ($d_z \\sim 1.0$) but a substantially weaker strategy $\\times$ phase interaction than in Qwen2-based architectures; random pruning also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient on LLaMA, reducing the strategy contrast. This partial confirmation ($s{=}0.30$ Wilcoxon $p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$; $s \\leq 0.25$ non-significant) suggests the qualitative pattern may generalize but quantitative impact is architecture-dependent. Comprehensive cross-architecture validation across more diverse designs (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention architectures) and scales remains an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark.\nDifferent reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.\nGeneralizing the phase-strategy interaction to broader reasoning domains is an important next step.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"Only MATH-500. How do we know this isn't a math-specific artifact?\"\n% REBUTTAL: The mechanism (entropy-discrimination collapse under head pruning) depends on attention distribution properties, not math-specific features. MATH-500 is a standard benchmark with diverse difficulty. However, different reasoning tasks may induce different phase structures—we state this limitation explicitly rather than overclaiming.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical scope.}\nBootstrap confidence intervals use $B{=}10{,}000$ resamples (recomputed from initial $B{=}1{,}000$ profiling runs). The paired permutation test yields $n{=}49$ paired problems for DeepSeek (1 excluded) and $n{=}46$ for Qwen (4 excluded). % exp_id: cohens_d_bf16.json, cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\nThe severity difference distribution is right-skewed (mean/median ratio ${\\sim}1.6{:}1$ at $s{=}0.30$), motivating our use of medians and nonparametric tests rather than parametric alternatives. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030; mean_diff=22.94, median_diff=14.59 (segment-level). Per-problem paired: median_diff=9.9436 at s030\nAll four permutation tests reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$; Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}), with CI lower bounds increasing from $0.19$ to $6.70$---confirming the effect direction at every sparsity level. The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification. % exp_id: permutation_test_results.json; permutation CIs: s015=[0.19,0.62], s020=[0.69,1.49], s025=[1.66,6.64], s030=[6.70,16.29]\n\nA notable exception among the random baselines is the Qwen random condition at $s{=}0.30$, which yields a statistically significant result ($p{=}0.012$) despite negligible effect size ($\\widetilde{\\Delta} = \\placeholder{}$, bootstrap CI spanning zero). % exp_id: qw_rand_s030; B=10000, p=0.012, d=-0.035, negligible\nThis case illustrates that dichotomous significance testing can flag effects of no practical magnitude---reinforcing the importance of reporting effect sizes and confidence intervals alongside $p$-values.\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity range.}\nOur experiments span $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$, a moderate range representative of practical deployment targets.\nMore extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.\nConversely, the lower boundary at which phase-modulated amplification first emerges is not yet characterized.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"Why not test higher sparsity? 0.30 is conservative.\"\n% REBUTTAL: Our focus is on the practically relevant regime where models retain usable quality. At s>0.30 both strategies degrade severely, making II measurement less informative. The sub-exponential growth (C6, $\\Delta$ growth rate $3.8{\\times} \\to 4.8{\\times} \\to 2.0{\\times}$) suggests saturation at higher sparsity, but confirming this is future work.\n\n\\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}\nWe do not report inference throughput or latency, which are necessary for deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"No system evaluation. How do we know the severity amplification matters for real deployment?\"\n% REBUTTAL: This is an empirical finding paper, not a method/system paper. The contribution is characterizing *what happens* (phase-modulated severity) and *why* (entropy-discrimination collapse), not building a system to fix it. Phase-gated controllers are explicitly scoped as future work. The finding is actionable regardless: any compound sparsity deployment should be aware of phase-dependent severity.\n\n% Entropy redistribution analysis moved to main text \\S\\ref{sec:entropy-redistribution}\n",

How do we know the severity amplification matters for real deployment?\"\n% REBUTTAL: This is an empirical finding paper, not a method/system paper. The contribution is characterizing *what happens* (phase-modulated severity) and *why* (entropy-discrimination collapse), not building a system to fix it. Phase-gated controllers are explicitly scoped as future work. The finding is actionable regardless: any compound sparsity deployment should be aware of phase-dependent severity.",
    "newString": "",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discrimination Collapse Details}\n\\label{app:discrimination-collapse-details}\n\nThis cross-model consistency (Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}) establishes discrimination collapse as a robust consequence of compound sparsity. When importance scores lose discriminative power, thresholding the bottom-$s$ fraction becomes noise-sensitive: small perturbations change which positions cross the eviction boundary. In the limit of perfectly uniform scores, importance-based eviction degenerates into an arbitrary selection functionally equivalent to biased random eviction. The same pattern holds under random eviction (DeepSeek: $-0.031$ to $-0.073$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.045$), confirming that discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \\emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\n\n\\section{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended-discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{On the predictability of phase-modulated severity.}\nThe compound interaction is not uniformly fragile---it is selectively fragile in a phase-dependent manner ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\text{high}} < 0.06$ at $s \\leq 0.30$). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; high-phase medians: 0.006, 0.015, 0.032, 0.053\n\n\\paragraph{Random masking as falsification control.}\nRandom eviction fails 7/8 paired permutation tests across both models; the single exception (Qwen $s{=}0.30$, $p{=}0.012$) exhibits negligible effect size ($\\widetilde{\\Delta} = \\placeholder{}$). The absence of a phase gradient under random masking isolates the amplification as strategy-specific. % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030, bf16_qw_rand_s015..s030\n\n\\paragraph{Entropy mechanism boundary conditions.}\nDirectly measuring per-head entropy shifts across architecturally distinct models---and identifying what training-time properties determine whether entropy concentrates or disperses under head pruning---would clarify the boundary conditions of the full mechanism.\n\n\n\\section{Extended Limitations}\n\\label{app:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Model scale.}\nAll experiments are conducted at the 7B parameter scale.\nWhether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested.\nLarger models may exhibit different attention redundancy structures that alter the discrimination collapse dynamics (and the model-dependent entropy component may behave differently at scale).\n\n\n\\paragraph{Architectural scope.}\nOur two models---DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct---share the Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nThe cross-model replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) establishes that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, though both models share the same Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nPreliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}) shows qualitatively consistent within-importance phase gradients ($d_z \\sim 1.0$) but a substantially weaker strategy $\\times$ phase interaction than in Qwen2-based architectures; random pruning also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient on LLaMA, reducing the strategy contrast. This partial confirmation ($s{=}0.30$ Wilcoxon $p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$; $s \\leq 0.25$ non-significant) suggests the qualitative pattern may generalize but quantitative impact is architecture-dependent. Comprehensive cross-architecture validation across more diverse designs (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention architectures) and scales remains an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark.\nDifferent reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.\nGeneralizing the phase-strategy interaction to broader reasoning domains is an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical scope.}\nBootstrap confidence intervals use $B{=}10{,}000$ resamples (recomputed from initial $B{=}1{,}000$ profiling runs). The paired permutation test yields $n{=}49$ paired problems for DeepSeek (1 excluded) and $n{=}46$ for Qwen (4 excluded). % exp_id: cohens_d_bf16.json, cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\nThe severity difference distribution is right-skewed (mean/median ratio ${\\sim}1.6{:}1$ at $s{=}0.30$), motivating our use of medians and nonparametric tests rather than parametric alternatives. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030; mean_diff=22.94, median_diff=14.59 (segment-level). Per-problem paired: median_diff=9.9436 at s030\nAll four permutation tests reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$; Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}), with CI lower bounds increasing from $0.19$ to $6.70$---confirming the effect direction at every sparsity level. The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification. % exp_id: permutation_test_results.json; permutation CIs: s015=[0.19,0.62], s020=[0.69,1.49], s025=[1.66,6.64], s030=[6.70,16.29]\n\nA notable exception among the random baselines is the Qwen random condition at $s{=}0.30$, which yields a statistically significant result ($p{=}0.012$) despite negligible effect size ($\\widetilde{\\Delta} = \\placeholder{}$, bootstrap CI spanning zero). % exp_id: qw_rand_s030; B=10000, p=0.012, d=-0.035, negligible\nThis case illustrates that dichotomous significance testing can flag effects of no practical magnitude---reinforcing the importance of reporting effect sizes and confidence intervals alongside $p$-values.\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity range.}\nOur experiments span $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$, a moderate range representative of practical deployment targets.\nMore extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.\nConversely, the lower boundary at which phase-modulated amplification first emerges is not yet characterized.\n\n\n\\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}\nWe do not report inference throughput or latency, which are necessary for deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"No system evaluation. How do we know the severity amplification matters for real deployment?\"\n% REBUTTAL: This is an empirical finding paper, not a method/system paper. The contribution is characterizing *what happens* (phase-modulated severity) and *why* (entropy-discrimination collapse), not building a system to fix it. Phase-gated controllers are explicitly scoped as future work. The finding is actionable regardless: any compound sparsity deployment should be aware of phase-dependent severity.\n\n% Entropy redistribution analysis moved to main text \\S\\ref{sec:entropy-redistribution}\n",
